## Page 1


STABILITY OF THE MINKOWSKI SPACETIME IN NEWMAN–UNTI GAUGE
JONATHAN LUK, SUNG-JIN OH, AND CLAUDE WARNICK
Abstract. We prove small-data global stability of the Minkowski solution to Einstein’s equations in
a centre-normalised outgoing null-geodesic gauge. Our scheme involves first using the rp-estimates of
Dafermos–Rodnianski to control certain components of the Weyl tensor which satisfy a decoupled tenso-
rial wave equation. Having established this control, all remaining geometric quantities are controlled by
transport equations, taking initial conditions at a regular central axis. This method establishes global
stability for initial data which decay only weakly to flat space and can establish additional asymptotic
control when the data are assumed to have more structure.
1. Introduction
In this paper, we investigate the global nonlinear asymptotic stability of the Minkowski spacetime as
a solution to the Einstein vacuum equations,
Ric(g) = 0.
(1.1)
This problem possesses a rich history. The first proof of stability was provided by Friedrich [Fri86] for
asymptotically hyperboloidal data. For the more general class of asymptotically flat data, stability was
first established in the monumental work of Christodoulou–Klainerman [CK93]. Subsequent develop-
ments have further refined our understanding, including works by Bieri [Bie10] and Shen [She23, She24a]
(weakening initial data decay assumptions), Lindblad–Rodnianski [LR05] (utilizing wave coordinates),
Hintz–Vasy [HV20] (establishing full asymptotic expansions at infinity in wave coordinates), and Ionescu–
Pausader [IP22] (developing a normal form analysis framework in wave coordinates). For a more com-
prehensive discussion of these and other prior works, we refer the reader to §1.3.1.
The main result of this paper is a new proof of stability of the Minkowski spacetime in coordinates
(u, r, θ1, θ2), with respect to which the metric takes the following form:
g = −(du ⊗dr + dr ⊗du) −fdu ⊗du + γAB(dθA −bAdu) ⊗(dθB −bBdu).
This choice, and the hierarchy of equations we shall use to control the solution, traces back to the work
of Newman–Unti [NU62] in 1962; for this reason, we refer to it as the Newman–Unti gauge. Its defining
features are that i) u is null, and ii) the coordinate vector field ∂r is null-geodesic. This gauge has a long
history in the physics literature (particularly, in the study of gravitational radiation), and it has also
appeared in several recent mathematical works, including [AC22, CK26, KS23].
What distinguishes our work from the above is a simple normalisation we adopt to fix the remaining
coordinate ambiguity, which we term centre-normalisation: starting with a fixed timelike geodesic Γ (the
centre), each level-u hypersurface Hu is the null cone generated by all outgoing null geodesics emanating
from the point on Γ with proper time u; for fixed u and θ, the curve r 7→(u, r, θ) represents each such null
generator with affine parametrisation; in particular, ∂r is null geodesic. (For the detailed construction,
see §4.) In Minkowski spacetime, if we consider the standard polar coordinates (t, r, θ), then {r = 0} is
the centre geodesic Γ, u = t −r is the standard retarded time, and the metric takes the form f = 1,
bA = 0, and γ = ˚γ, where ˚γ is the standard metric on the round sphere of radius r.
Given the existing literature, one might ask: Why another proof of the stability of the Minkowski
spacetime? As already noted in [NU62], the analytic structure of (1.1) in the Newman–Unti gauge is
particularly elegant: the entire dynamics of (1.1) is represented by the curvature components αAB =
R(∂r, ∂θA, ∂r, ∂θB), which, to linear order around Minkowski, solves a decoupled wave equation (Teukol-
sky equation). Under our centre-normalisation, all metric components are then recovered by integrating
the fundamental equations of geometry (i.e., Bianchi and null structure equations) along the generators
of the null cones Hu starting from the centre Γ.
Building upon this simple hierarchical structure, our primary objective is to develop a streamlined
framework for proving stability. Our proof is concise and encompasses the entire1 spectrum of initial
1The only result in the literature whose decay assumption we do not handle seems to be that of Shen [She24a], which
handles the endpoint case in the setting of exterior stability (i.e., in the domain of dependence of the exterior of a compact
set).

decay assumptions of interest, ranging from the optimal (up to the endpoint) weak decay assumption of
Shen [She23], to the strongly decaying data originally considered by Christodoulou–Klainerman, to new
classes of further structured data that lead to the almost sharp Bondi–Sachs peeling. Furthermore, our
framework readily extends to settings such as exterior stability [KN03a] and the spacelike-characteristic
initial value problem [Gra20] (see §A for further discussion).
In addition, the centre-normalised Newman–Unti gauge turns out to be exceptionally well-suited for
studying global asymptotics. This utility is perhaps surprising, given that our gauge is normalised at the
centre Γ rather than future infinity (see also the discussion of divergent behaviour of metric coefficients
below). Nonetheless, in this gauge, all geometric quantities we consider exhibit simple asymptotic be-
haviour as u →+∞(referred to as late-time tails). Our stability proof shows that for solutions with
weakly decaying initial data, all curvature components obey the global upper bound O(ϵ2r−1−⟨u⟩−1+
),
where ϵ2 is the initial data size and ⟨·⟩:= (1+(·)2)
1
2 . On the other extreme, for solutions exhibiting almost
sharp Bondi–Sachs peeling, all curvature components have a decay rate of at least O(ϵ2r−1−⟨u+⟩−4),
where (·)+ := max{(·), 0}; this is a new result in this generality. Consistent with the hierarchical struc-
ture of the equations, the Ricci (or connection) coefficients and metric components exhibit the same
upper bound, but multiplied by r and r2, respectively.
Furthermore, combined with the methods of Luk–Oh [LO24], the centre-normalised Newman–Unti
gauge allows for the proof of sharp Bondi–Sachs peeling for general strongly asymptotically flat initial
data (whose precise formulation is an appropriate extension of Definition 1.3). One may additionally
conjecture that in this gauge, the generic local-in-space decay rate is u−6 for the curvature components
and r2u−6 for the metric components, consistent with the modified Price’s law conjecture of Luk–Oh
[LO24]. This would be markedly different from the case of wave coordinates [Lin17, Mao26].
Despite these advantages, the usefulness of the centre-normalised Newman–Unti gauge for proving
stability may seem doubtful at first glance. First, there is an apparent loss of derivatives in this gauge
[CK26] so that different connection coefficients appear to have different regularity.
More seriously,
integrating the equations from the centre reveals that, no matter how fast αAB is assumed to decay in
r, the metric components diverge(!) from the Minkowskian values as r →∞:
f −1 = O(r),
|b| = O(r),
|γ −˚γ| = O(1)
on each fixed Hu.
(1.2)
Our key new insight is that neither the loss of derivatives nor the divergence of the metric components
obstruct sharp wave equation estimates for αAB on this background. Indeed, (1.2) notwithstanding, the
estimates are accompanied by enough u-decay and smallness, which we justify via a suitable bootstrap
argument, so that they can still be treated perturbatively. This observation represents a new ramification
of the remarkable null structure of the Einstein equations, a classical theme that underlies the proof of
Christodoulou–Klainerman [CK93] and all subsequent works. We refer to §1.2 below for a more detailed
discussion of the main ideas of the proof.
Remark 1.1. The Newman–Unti gauge is closely related to the Bondi–Metzner–Sachs (BMS) gauge
often used in the physics literature to describe the precise asymptotics of asymptotically flat spacetimes
[Bon60, BvdBM62, Sac62]. Indeed, the definitions of the two gauges differ only by the choice of the
‘radial’ coordinate r: it is an affine parameter on generators of Hu in the Newman–Unti gauge, and it is
the areal coordinate in the BMS gauge. However, we point out that both gauges used in this context are
typically normalised at future infinity, which is different from our centre-normalisation; in particular,
the metric behaviour in (1.2) is precluded.

Remark 1.4.
i) The slowly decaying spatial tails, such as 2M
r δij, are necessitated for large values of
ν by the Einstein vacuum constraint equations (1.3). Indeed, the parameter M in Definition 1.3.ii)–
iii) is the ADM mass, which is strictly positive for any non-Minkowskian data by the Positive Mass
Theorem [SY81, Wit81].
ii) While structurally necessary, the precise form of these slowly decaying tails is not unique; our defini-
tion is based on physical and mathematical considerations. Specifically, Definition 1.3.ii) corresponds
to the strongly asymptotically flat condition originally implemented by Christodoulou–Klainerman
[CK90] and utilised in ensuing works. The terms in Definition 1.3.iii) correspond to the asymptotic
form of the metric far from a localised gravitating system, as found in Misner–Thorne–Wheeler
[MTW73, (19.13)]; alternatively, they match the asymptotics of the Cauchy data for the Kerr space-
time on constant Boyer–Lindquist time slices expressed in suitable spatial coordinates (see the proof
of Corollary B.3). In this context, the parameter J denotes the ADM angular momentum.
iii) In all cases we consider, the ADM linear momentum P and the ADM centre-of-mass C, whenever
they are well-defined, are fixed to be zero. This property signifies that the Cauchy data are posed in
the centre-of-mass frame.
iv) The existence of a large class of Cauchy data in Definition 1.3.ii)–iii) follows from the recent works
of Fang–Szeftel–Touati [FST25] and Chen–Klainerman [CK25]. In fact, the Cauchy data in Defini-
tion 1.3 is general in the small-data regime in the following sense: for every 0 < ν < 3, any small
Cauchy data of the aforementioned form can be parameterised – up to a Poincar´e transformation
to pass to the centre-of-mass frame – by the linear space of all solutions to the linearised constraint

1.2.1. Transport equations along e4. As observed originally by Newman–Unti [NU62], given the curvature
components α it is possible to recover all coefficients describing the geometry of (M, g) by integrating
transport equations along e4. For instance, the following chain of transport equations – arising from the
second Bianchi identity and structure equations – may be used to recover the metric coefficient f:

The general strategy of combining wave estimates for α and transport estimates after choosing a
suitable gauge is reminiscent of an essential step in the proof of black hole stability [DHR19, DHRT21,
GKS24, KS23]. Notice, however, in all those works wave estimates were needed for both the curvature
components α and α, and, more importantly, the gauge is normalised to the future at infinity, in constrast
to the centre normalisation that we use.
1.2.2. Wave estimates for the Teukolsky equation. As is well-known, the second Bianchi identity and the
Einstein vacuum equation imply second-order tensorial wave equations for curvature components. In
particular, for the components αAB, we have the equation

the strength of the weight w near the centre, whose discussion we postpone until §1.2.4. Essentially
the same computation shows that, for p∞in the range (1.10), p0 in another suitable range (see (8.10)
below), and a > 0 suitably small, then we still have the estimate (1.11).
We remark that turning the r-weight into a corresponding (r+⟨u⟩)-weight is analogous to the dyadic
pigeonhole argument in [DR09]. However, in our case, we use a simple alternative with a weight that
directly grows in u (with a smallness parameter a > 0), which works well with centre normalisation.

To prove Theorem 1.5, we utilise estimates (1.11) and (1.12) with p∞= δ. Indeed, one may already
observe that integrating the transport operator in the first term of (1.11) leads to an L2-estimate for α
on each sphere Su,r that would lead to α = O(ϵ2(r + ⟨u⟩)−2−δ) (assumed in §1.2.1) after we commute
with S−tangential gradients and apply Sobolev embeddings on Su,r (see §§1.2.3, 1.2.5). For almost sharp
Bondi–Sachs peeling, we use p∞= 3 −δ. We defer the discussion of the choice p0 until §1.2.4.
We end the discussion on the estimates for the Teukolsky equation with a few more remarks:
Remark 1.7.
i) In contrast to the classical vector field method of Christodoulou–Klainerman [CK90],
there is no need for the construction of any time-like approximately (conformal) Killing vector fields
to be used as multipliers. See also Remark 1.8.ii) for a related comment in the context of commuting
vector fields.
ii) The greater range of p in (1.10) than one expects for the scalar wave equation (see [DR09]) reflects
the more favourable properties of the Teukolsky equation from the point of view of rp estimates.
iii) In contrast to the original [DR09], our rp estimate closes without relying on a separate integrated
local energy decay (or Morawetz) estimate. We note that a conceptually similar strategy was re-
cently implemented by Gautam [Gau26] in the context of two-dimensional wave equations, where the
standard Morawetz estimate is known to fail due to poor low-frequency behaviour.

7
1.2.3. Higher order derivatives and closing the transport-wave estimates. Next, we turn to the issue of
deriving estimates for higher order derivatives of α, and discuss how one avoids possible derivative losses
when finally closing the e4-transport and wave estimates.
Our main commuting operators are r /∇and r /∇4, which are weighted derivatives tangent to each
Hu. Let s0 be the maximum number of such operators we commute the Teukolsky equation in our
argument, and let ϕ represent (r /∇4)i(r /∇)jα for any i + j ⩽s0. Using an observation going back to
[DR09] and expanded in [Sch13, Mos16], estimates analogous to (1.11) and (1.12) can be also proved
for (r /∇4)i(r /∇)jα, provided that we have an adequate control of (r /∇4)i′(r /∇)j′ applied to the right-hand
side of (1.9) for all i′ + j′ ⩽s0.
In particular, in order to close all the estimates, we need to control appropriate orders of derivatives
of the curvature components that arise on the right-hand side of (1.9); specifically, we need s0 + 1 many
derivatives of β, and s0 many derivatives of σ and K, the Gauss curvature of the spheres Su,r (see §8 for
details). From (1.11) and (1.12) for the derivatives of α and simply using e4-transport equations as in
§1.2.1, we control exactly s0 derivatives for β and s0−1 derivatives of σ and K, which are insufficient. To
overcome such possible derivative losses for curvature components, we utilise the following observations:
- The second Bianchi identity relates /∇4β and /∇b⊗β (the symmetric traceless part of /∇β) with
/
divα
and /∇3α, respectively, which are controlled with up to s0 many derivatives in the weighted spacetime
norms in (1.11) and (1.12) (see (2.40) and (2.38), respectively). Combined with an elliptic estimate
on spheres to control /∇β via /∇b⊗β, we may control s0 + 1 many derivatives of β. Observe that at
the top order, as for wave equations in general, not all derivatives can be controlled on the null Hu
hypersurfaces. As a result, we also use integrated spacetime norms to control certain top derivatives
of β, as well as other quantities that depend on it.
- Once we estimate s0 + 1 derivatives of β, we also control s0 derivatives for σ and K. For σ, this is
immediate from the transport equation; for K, we use the Gauss equation (see (2.37)) to relate it
directly with the null curvature component ρ, which is controlled in exactly the same manner as σ.
Observe from the above discussion that not all curvature components are controlled at the same level of
regularity, in contrast to more standard approaches which view the Bianchi equations as a hyperbolic
system and treat all curvature components at the same level. Importantly, however, this is consistent
with what is need to close the estimates for the Teukolsky equation.
One must also consider the possibility of derivative losses arising from Ricci coefficients, whose discus-
sion we have suppressed so far. Using the transport equations for the Ricci coefficients, one is naturally
led to estimates with different orders of derivatives for different Ricci coefficients. Specifically (see defi-
nitions in §2.2), after using the improvements for the curvature components discussed above, we control
s0 + 1, s0 and s0 −1 derivatives of (χ, η), (ω, χ) and ξ, respectively. Remarkably, this hierarchy of
regularity is still consistent with the derivative count needed for the Teukolsky estimates.
The estimates derived so far allow for further commutation of the Teukolsky equation with ⟨u⟩/∇u,
where /∇u := 1
2( /∇3 + f /∇4), but at a lower order. In effect, we may ‘trade’ two copies of r /∇4, r /∇with
one ⟨u⟩/∇u. We remark that the commutation with ⟨u⟩/∇u is not needed to close the a-priori estimates
above for the e4-transport and Teukolsky equations, but it is used to ensure that the spacetime can be
smoothly continued (in a different coordinate chart) near the centre; see §1.2.4.
We end this discussion with two more remarks:
Remark 1.8.
i) The omitted nonlinear terms in the transport equations and the Teukolsky equation
(1.9) possess several important properties. As mentioned above, the Ricci coefficients appear in a
manner that incur no derivative losses (after using the elliptic estimates discussed above). Moreover,
the nonlinear terms omit the worst (from the point of view of decay) potential nonlinear interactions.
This specific null structure, combined with the smallness and u-decay of the metric deviations, en-
sures that the nonlinearities can be treated as error terms in our scheme. For a detailed verification,
we refer the reader to §8 and §9 below for the Teukolsky and transport equations, respectively.
ii) We point out that r /∇A and ⟨u⟩/∇u serve as good commuting operators within our scheme, even
though the vector fields reA and 1
2(e3 + fe4) may not even be asymptotically Killing as r →+∞
(indeed, the 33-component of the deformation tensor of both may grow as O(ϵr)).
1.2.4. Behaviour near the centre. So far, we have suppressed the discussion of what happens near the
centre. Centre-normalisation necessarily leads to non-smooth estimates near the centre; indeed, α itself
cannot be smooth near Γ since e4 is not regular. Our approach is to show that the spacetime is never-
theless smooth near Γ (in a different coordinate chart than Newman–Unti) by establishing a control on g
that is sufficient for the application of the classical (i.e., relying only on energy estimates) low-regularity
local well-posedness result, which implies the desired smoothness via persistence of regularity.

1.2.5. Equivalence of norms. With our estimates, we are able to always establish the equivalence between
the transport/wave estimates discussed above – which come naturally with norms and derivatives asso-
ciated with the metric g – with norms and derivatives defined using the background Minkowski metric;
see §6.1. In this fashion, we avoid the need to prove geometric versions of standard analytic inequalities,
such as Sobolev embeddings and interpolation.
1.3. Related works.
1.3.1. Literature on the stability of the Minkowski spacetime. In order to highlight where our approach
differs from previous works, we very crudely summarise aspects of past results on stability of Minkowski
as a solution of the Einstein-vacuum equations in Table 1.
Taking the main points of comparison in turn, we first note the character of the surface on which the
initial data is prescribed. Our result assumes data on a spacelike surface, however since the gauge is
very well adapted to outgoing null cones the results can be adapted with minimal changes to either the
initial-characteristic problem (with data for α prescribed on an outgoing cone) or the problem exterior
to a sphere, see Appendix A. Note that in the second case the result will necessarily be semi-global as
I+ will not be complete.
We next record the broad approach to gauge fixing taken within each work. The approach of [Fri86] is
somewhat sui generis, relying on conformal techniques to render the problem local-in-time. The remaining
works divide roughly into two camps: the more geometric approach pioneered by Christodoulou and
Klainerman in which the primary estimates are performed at the level of curvature; and the approach
based on studying the wave equations satisfied by the metric components in harmonic coordinates due
to Lindblad–Rodnianski. The advantage of the first is that the null structure of Einstein’s equations
is more directly evident. The second approach connects more directly to the theory of nonlinear wave


## Page 9


STABILITY OF MINKOWSKI
9
equations, and can draw on that literature. Our gauge is geometric, but unlike previous works which
require more than one foliation, we gain in efficiency by only considering a single null foliation.
Next we note the decay rates assumed in each work. Since different norms are used in the various
works to describe the asymptotic decay precisely, the parameter ν recorded here should be understood
in a loose sense. Where the initial data surface does not extend to i0, the value of ν is inferred from the
decay of α near I+. Our approach enables us to both establish the optimal decay of [She23], but also to
show stronger peeling results as in [KN03b].
Finally we list the mechanism by which the main dynamical estimates are proved in each case. By
‘classical vf estimates’ we refer to results obtained within the general paradigm of the vector field method,
[CK90], making use of the conformal symmetries of Minkowski. By rp estimates we refer to results in
the framework of the ‘new method’ of Dafermos–Rodnianski [DR09]. Our use of the rp-method is well
adapted to the null foliation as it avoids the need to construct approximate conformal Killing fields.
1.3.2. Stability of Minkowski space in the presence of matter fields. There have also been many works
on the stability of Minkowski as a solution of the Einstein equations coupled to various matter models.
We mention here [LM16, Wan20, IP22] (Klein–Gordon); [ZY00, Loi09] (Maxwell); [Spe10] (nonlinear
electromagnetism); [KL23] (Maxwell–Klein–Gordon); [LT20, FJS21, Wan22] (massive Vlasov); [Tay17,
BFJ+21] (massless Vlasov); [Hun18, HSW23, ABWY23] (Kaluza–Klein).
1.3.3. Other related works. (Exterior stability) There are other setups for which the stability of Minkowski
is studied. One setting is the stability in the domain of dependence of the exterior of a compact set in
the Cauchy data, first proven in [KN03a]. See also [Hin23, She23, She24b].
(Scattering problem) Another natural setting for stability is the full scattering problem, where initial
data are posed on past null infinity. Near spacelike infinity, this has been recently resolved in [KK25].
(Regularity of future null infinity) A related problem concerns the regularity of future null infinity,
and in particular whether peeling occurs; see [Chr02, Fri18, KN03b, Keh22, N¨u25] for discussions.
(Spherically symmetric problems) Prior to the works [Fri86, CK93], the stability of Minkowski space-
time was first proven in spherical symmetry for the Einstein–scalar field system [Chr86]. In that setting,
Christodoulou also established a scale-invariant result [Chr93]; an alternative setting containing some
settings beyond scale-invariant data can be found in [LOY18]. Both of these results could be compared
to the ν = 0+ case in Table 1. See also [RR92, Daf06] for the Einstein–Vlasov system.
(Stability of dispersive spacetimes) Beyond Minkowski spacetime itself, large-data solutions which
disperse to Minkowski spacetime are also known to be stable [LO22].
Finally, we refer the reader to the surveys [She26, Smu25] for other related works and further details.
1.4. Organisation of the paper. The remainder of the paper will be organised as follows. The next
few sections contain preliminaries for our setup. In §2, we will discuss the geometry associated to the
Newman–Unti gauge and write down the Einstein equations in this gauge. In §3, we then introduce the
norms that we use in this paper. In §4 we describe how to construct Newman–Unti coordinates starting
from a Cauchy surface. In §5, we give the assumptions on the initial data and bootstrap assumptions, and
precisely state the main theorem. Our proof is based on a bootstrap argument, and we begin the proof
in §6, §7 by establishing some immediate consequences of the bootstrap assumptions and establishing
bounds on solutions to transport equations. In §8 we analyse the Teukolsky equation, establishing the
necessary linear and nonlinear estimates.
The estimates for the other geometric quantities are then
proven in §9. Finally, we define the bootstrap region in §10, prove regularity near the centre and future
completeness in §11, and conclude the proof in §12. In Appendix A we show how to adapt our proof
to the case of exterior stability and the spacelike-characteristic initial value problem. In Appendix B
we connect the initial data assumptions in Definition 1.3 and Theorem 5.6 to those used in the main
bootstrap argument formulated in §5.

2.6. Differential operators.
Definition 2.7. We say that a tensor field defined on U is S−tangent if it coincides with its g-orthogonal
projection to Su,r.
For example a 2-tensor field T defined on U is S−tangent if
T3A = TA3 = T4A = TA4 = T34 = 0
for A = 1, 2. We can identify an S−tangent tensor field with its components in the usual way - in the
example above we would identify T with the components TAB, which are functions of u, r, θA. We can
raise and lower S−tangent indices with γAB and γAB := (γ−1)AB.

3. Regions, surfaces and norms
In order to treat the behaviour of the metric at the axis of the coordinate system, we shall work
on R4 with an axis ‘blown-up’. On the manifold with boundary Ru × [0, ∞)r × S2
θ with its canonical
differentiable structure we define the function t = u + r and let S be the region
S = {(u, r, θ) ∈Ru × [0, ∞)r × S2
θ : t ⩾1}
Let τ = τ(u, r) be the function constructed in §10. For T > 1 we define the following (sub-)manifolds:
Definition 3.1 (Subsets of the spacetime).
ST := {(u, r, θ) ∈S : τ < T},
(3.1)
Στ ′ := {(u, r, θ) ∈ST : τ = τ ′},
(3.2)
˜Σt′ := {(u, r, θ) ∈ST : t = t′},
(3.3)
Su′,r′ := {(u, r, θ) ∈ST : u = u′, r = r′},
(3.4)
Hu′ := {(u, r, θ) ∈ST : u = u′}.
(3.5)
The initial data surface will be ˜Σ1. We note that for 1 < τ ′ < T the surface Στ ′ meets ˜Σ1. Under our
bootstrap assumptions, away from r = 0, ˜Σ1 will be a smooth spacelike surface, and Στ ′ a C1,1, piecewise
smooth, spacelike surface (see Figure 1, p54). Let uT be the unique solution to τ(uT , 1 −uT ) = T so
that u = uT on ΣT ∩˜Σ1.

4. Construction of coordinates
Suppose that (Σ, h, k) is a smooth set of Cauchy data for the vacuum Einstein equations, with Σ ≃R3.
In this section, we carry out the construction of centre-normalised Newman–Unti gauge.
4.1. The centre geodesic and centre-normalisation. Let O ∈Σ be a marked point. By assumption
we can cover Σ by a single coordinate chart Φ : Σ →R3 such that without loss of generality O corresponds
to the origin and hij(0) = δij.
Let θ = (θ
A) be a local6 choice of coordinates on the unit sphere
S2 ⊂R3 and ˆx : θ →S2 the corresponding chart.
We define r =

(r, θ) 7→Φ−1(rˆx(θ)) is a coordinate chart on Σ \ O. Identifying θ with the tangent vector to the curve
λ 7→Φ−1(λˆx(θ)) at λ = 0 we have that θ induces a canonical choice of coordinates θ 7→R(θ) ∈SOΣ ⊂
TOΣ.
Now we let (M, g, ι) be the maximal smooth development of the data (Σ, h, k), i.e., a 4−manifold M
equipped with a Ricci flat, Lorentzian metric g together with an embedding ι : Σ →M such that h, k
are respectively the corresponding first and second fundamental form and all objects are smooth. We
wish to construct Newman–Unti coordinates in a neighbourhood of ι(Σ). Let N be the future directed
unit normal to ι(Σ). There exists some δu > 0 such that the geodesic Γ : (1−δu, 1+δu) →M satisfying
Γ(1) = ι(O), ˙Γ(1) = N exists; this is the centre geodesic, or the spacetime axis. Given θA we define the
null vector V (θ) ∈Tι(O)M by V (1, θ) = N + ι∗R(θ) and extend this to a vector field V (u, θ) along Γ by
parallel transport.
We may think of (u, θ) 7→V (u, θ) as a smooth embedding V : (1−δu, 1+δu)×S2 →TM. From each
point V (u, θ) we follow the geodesic flow on TM a parameter distance r, and label this point Ψ(u, r, θ).
By construction, possibly after shrinking δu, we have that Ψ : (1 −δu, 1 + δu) × [0, δr) × S2 →TM is
well-defined and smooth for some 0 < δr < δu.
To establish that Ψ is an embedding (possibly after again shrinking the domain) it suffices to show
that Ψ∗(∂u),Ψ∗(∂r),Ψ∗(∂θA) span a 4−dimensional subspace in TΨ(u,r,θ)TM for each (u, r, θ). To see
this, let γu,θ : [0, r0) →M be the geodesic with γu,θ(0) = Γ(u), ˙γu,θ(0) = V (u, θ). If Π : TM →M is
the canonical projection, then γu,θ(r) = Π ◦Ψ(u, r, θ). We have that

Remark 4.1. The construction above, which ‘blows-up’ the axis Γ to the cylinder {r = 0}, enables us to
cleanly discuss regularity at Γ in terms of smooth objects on a manifold with boundary. This is mainly
for convenience to avoid having to express regularity in terms of limits as r →0.

We summarise this discussion in the following theorem
Theorem 4.2. Given (Σ, h, k) a smooth set of Cauchy data for the vacuum Einstein equations, with
Σ ≃R3 and O ∈Σ a marked point, let (M, g, ι) be its maximal Cauchy development, and Γ the future
directed geodesic normal to ι(Σ) at O, parameterised by proper time. Given δr > 0 sufficiently small
there exists U, open in S, a neighbourhood of ˜Σ1 and a smooth map Ψ : U →TM such that
i) Π ◦Ψ : U \ {r = 0} →M \ Γ is a smooth diffeomorphism onto its image

5. Bootstrap assumptions and statement of the main theorem
We fix s0 ⩾20 to be even (see footnote 2). Fix some δ > 0 which we will later choose sufficiently small
(δ < 1/20 will suffice). For compactness of notation we set p± := p ± δ. We will later take 0 < ϵ0 ≪1
to be sufficiently small depending on s0 and δ. From now on, we assume that 0 < ϵ ⩽ϵ0.

Remark 5.1. In this case, the exponents p0, p∞are determined entirely by the object type: for curvature
components, (p0, p∞) = ((−1)+, 2+); for connection coefficients, (p0, p∞) = (0+, 1+); and for metric
components, (p0, p∞) = (1+, 0+). The exponents s match with those in our bootstrap assumptions (5.8)
and (5.11) below.

Remark 5.3 (Cancellation properties of the leading order asymptotics in Definition 1.3). The validity
of the strong initial data assumptions (5.4), (5.5) and (5.6) (specifically the control on α and β) implies
nontrivial cancellation properties of the leading-order spatial asymptotics prescribed in Definition 1.3.iii).
Naively, a generic r−1 term in the metric tensor would typically induce r−3 terms in the curvature tensor,
which would be inconsistent with the p∞exponents for the norms of α and β in these assumptions. Indeed,
they reflect the fact that the initial data for β is entirely devoid of terms below order r−4, and also that
α does not have terms below order r−5−.
In Appendix B, we verify this cancellation by showing that the leading-order spatial asymptotics in
Definition 1.3 are exactly those of an initial data set for the Kerr metric. The problem then reduces to
checking that (5.4), (5.5) and (5.6) hold for the exact Kerr metric when equipped with our Newman–Unti
gauge and restricted outside a large ball, which can be done by explicit computation. We further note
that if J = 0 (corresponding to the Schwarzschild metric), there are more cancellations: both β and α
completely vanish for the exact Schwarzschild metric equipped with our Newman–Unti gauge (adapted to
symmetry spheres).

Remark 5.5. Note that the s and p0 exponents are the same as the weak bootstrap case; the only
difference lies in the p∞exponents.
All p∞exponents in (5.10) are sharp in view of the structure
of the transport equation the geometric quantities satisfy (dictated by λ0 in Theorem 7.1 below), with
the exception of α (for which p∞= 5−is δ away from the sharp value 5). On the other hand, the
values of p∞in (5.11) are mostly determined by the initial data assumptions, specifically, (5.6) for
α, β, ˆχ, /trχ −2r−1, η, ρ, σ and (5.3) for ˆχ, /trχ + 2r−1, f −2ωr −1. For the remaining quantity K −r−2,
p∞is determined through the others by the constraint equation (2.37) (see also (9.15)).
5.3. Precise formulation of the main theorem. We are now in a position to precisely state the main
result of the paper.
Theorem 5.6. Fix s0, δ as above. Suppose (Σ, h, k) is a smooth set of Cauchy data for the vacuum
Einstein equations with Σ ≃R3, with maximal future Cauchy development (M, g, ι). Choose Newman–
Unti coordinates in a neighbourhood of ι(Σ) by the process described in §4. There exists ϵ0 = ϵ0(s0, δ) > 0
such that if that the resulting metric satisfies the weak initial data assumptions (5.1), (5.2) for 0 < ϵ < ϵ0
the following conclusions hold.
i) (M, g) is globally smooth and future complete and may be covered by a single Newman–Unti coordi-
nate chart, regular away from the axis.
ii) (M, g) is asymptotically flat in the sense that all curvature components with respect to the frame
(eA, e3, e4) decay to zero along any inextendible future-directed causal curve.
iii) The metric components, the Ricci coefficients, and the curvature components satisfy the bounds
(5.8), (5.9) globally, with ϵ replaced by Cϵ2 for some C = C(s0, δ) > 0.
Furthermore, there exists ϵ′
0 = ϵ0(s0, δ) > 0 such that if the metric satisfies the strong initial data
assumptions (5.1), (5.2), (5.4) and (5.5) for 0 < ϵ < ϵ′
0 then the bounds (5.10), (5.11) hold globally,
again with ϵ replaced by Cϵ2. In particular, the spacetime exhibits the almost sharp Bondi–Sachs peeling
property, in the sense that (1.7) holds.
We defer the proof until §12.

7. Transport equations
Under the bootstrap assumptions, we now establish estimates for the transport equation along e4.
The main result is as follows:
Theorem 7.1. For any fixed choice of indices for the norms below, there exists ϵ0 sufficiently small such
that the following holds whenever the weak bootstrap assumptions (5.8)–(5.9) hold for ϵ ⩽ϵ0. Suppose ϕ,

9. Estimates for the other geometric quantities
In this section, we start with the estimates for α in Corollary 8.13 and prove estimates for all the
other geometric quantities. In particular, we improve the bootstrap assumptions (5.8)–(5.11).
We first consider the bounds on the 2-sphere.
Proposition 9.1.
(1) Assume that the weak bootstrap assumptions hold. Assume in addition that
α obeys the following bounds:
|α|s0,(−1)+,2+ ≲ϵ2.
(9.1)
Then, for ϵ0 > 0 sufficiently small, in fact the bounds in the bootstrap assumptions (5.8) hold
with ϵ replaced by Cϵ2.

Proof. We prove the desired bounds using the transport estimate (7.1) in Theorem 7.1 and the initial
data bound (5.2), plus (5.5) for the strong bootstrap. There are four things to check: (1) total number
of derivatives, (2) weights near r = 0, (3) weights near r = ∞, and (4) the smallness constant.
Let us first discuss the smallness constant. We control the terms in an order such that all the error
terms are either quadratic (so that the term is of size ϵ2 by the bootstrap assumption) or are linear terms
that have been controlled in the previous step (so that the term is again of size ϵ2 because this has been
proven).
The sequence in which we choose to estimate the geometric quantities is as follows:
α →(ˆχ, /trχ, β) →(γ, η, ρ, σ, K) →(b, ˆχ, /trχ, ω, β) →(f, ξ),
(9.3)
meaning that when estimating ˆχ, /trχ, or β, the only linear terms are given in terms of α; when estimating
γ, η, ρ, or σ, the linear terms only involve α, ˆχ, /trχ, or β, etc. This structure can be readily observed in
the equations.
We now consider (1)–(3) for each of the quantities involved.
Here is the procedure that we will go through. We use the transport estimate (7.1) with parameters
(s, λ0, q0, p∞) satisfying the following admissibility conditions which are required in Theorem 7.1:
2λ0 + q0 + 1 > 0,
p∞⩽2λ0.
(9.4)
The parameters will also be chosen so that the initial data term arising in (7.1) will be bounded by (5.2)
and (5.5).

Remark 9.3 (Propagation of Bondi–Sachs peeling, II). Together with Remark 8.14, the purpose of
this remark is to provide more details of the propagation of Bondi–Sachs peeling in the context of the
spacelike-characteristic initial value problem alluded to in Remark A.3. Observe that the modified boot-
strap assumption (8.23) follows from the same argument as in Step 3 of the preceding proposition. Here,
the important difference is that the initial data for the transport estimates are only needed on Σ = B1(0),
which allows us to use q∞not permitted by (5.6) in the Cauchy data case.

11. Control of geodesics
11.1. Geodesic surfaces emanating from the axis. Fix u0 ∈(1, T). Let ˙Γ(u0)⊥= {v ∈TΓ(u0)M :
g(v, ˙Γ(u0)) = 0} be the linear subspace of directions orthogonal to the axis at Γ(u0).
Taking U ⊂
˙Γ(u0)⊥a sufficiently small open neighbourhood of the origin, it follows from elementary properties of
the exponential map that expΓ(u0) : U →M is a smooth embedding, and that N = expΓ(u0)(U) ⊂M is
a smooth spacelike hypersurface. In this section we prove:

˙Γ(u0)⊥: g(v, v) < 1/2}. Then N = N(u0) = expΓ(u0)(U) is a smoothly embedded submanifold for as
long as it remains in the image of ST . Moreover, if h, k are the first and second fundamental forms of

11.2. Future completeness.
Lemma 11.11. Suppose (M, g) is a smooth solution of Einstein’s equations as above, such that after
constructing Newman–Unti coordinates the weak bootstrap assumptions hold in S. Then (M, g) is future
geodesically complete.
Proof. We need to show that if γ : [0, λ0) →M is a future-directed causal geodesic with λ0 < ∞, then
it can be extended. In S we write γ(λ) = (u(λ), r(λ), θA(λ)) and
˙γ(λ) = /vAea + v3e3 + v4e4,
where u, r, θA are piecewise smooth, with isolated discontinuities when the geodesic passes through the
axis10, at which points u, ˙u, r are continuous and ˙r, θA have finite discontinuities. The geodesic equations
are (11.1), (11.9), (11.10), (11.11). The condition that γ is future directed and causal implies v3, v4 ⩾0.
In view of the fact that g is smooth, the only way that γ can fail to be extendable is if either r(λ) or
u(λ) tends to infinity as λ ↗λ0, so it suffices to show that ˙u, ˙r are bounded.

12. Proof of the main theorem

this argument (for a possibly smaller ϵ0) using the strong bootstrap assumptions (5.10), (5.11) in addition
to (5.8), (5.9).

Appendix A. Localising the main stability theorem
In this appendix, we formulate the theorems regarding exterior stability of Minkowski and the stability
for mixed Cauchy-characteristic data. Both results involve a simple localisation of our main theorem.
As mentioned in the introduction, these results generalise those in [KN03a] and [Gra20]. We begin with
the exterior stability result.
Corollary A.1 (Exterior nonlinear stability of Minkowski spacetime). Let (Σ, h, k) be a smooth set of
Cauchy data for the Einstein vacuum equations such that for a compact set K ⊂Σ, Σ\K is diffeomorphic
to R3\BR(0) for some R > 0 and thus equipped with coordinates x1, x2, x3. Suppose that for some s ⩾30
and ν > 0, (Σ \ K, h, k) satisfies

i) Let eK ⊇K be the compact set such that Σ \ eK corresponds to R3 \ B e
R(0) under the diffeomorphism
above. Then the maximal future Cauchy development (M, g) of Σ \ eK may be covered by a single
Newman–Unti coordinate chart, and the outgoing null hypersurface emanating from ∂eK (given as
the constant {u = 1 −eR} hypersurface in the Newman–Unti coordinates) is a future null boundary
of the spacetime.
ii) There exists ϵ > 0 such that the estimates (5.8), (5.9) hold in (M, g).
iii) If, in addition, (A.1) holds with ν = 3 −δ, then after choosing eR larger, the estimates (5.10), (5.11)
hold for some ϵ > 0.

We next formulate the result for stability of Minkowski spacetime for the spacelike-characteristic
initial value problem. The data are given on a spacelike hypersurface Σ = B1(0) and a characteristic
hypersurface H0 which share a boundary diffeomorphic to S2. On Σ we prescribe the usual Cauchy data,
but observe that we only need to prescribe characteristic data for γ on H0. The other components, i.e.,
b and f, as well as the other geometric quantities depending on the transversal derivatives of the metric
components (e.g., χ and some curvature components), are determined by the corresponding transport
equation and the initial data computed from (Σ, h, k).
Corollary A.2 (Stability for the spacelike-characteristic initial value problem). Let s ∈Z⩾30. Consider
the spacelike-characteristic initial value problem satisfying the following conditions:

There exists eC = eC(s, s0, δ) > 0 such that if nΣ + nH < eC, then the following conclusions hold for
some C = C(s, s0, δ) > 0.
i) The maximal future Cauchy development (M, g) is globally smooth and future complete and may be
covered by a single Newman–Unti chart, which is regular away from the centre geodesic.
ii) The estimates (5.8), (5.9) hold in (M, g) with ϵ replaced by C(nΣ + nH).

Proof. We will explain how to construct a local solution under the given assumptions. The estimates we
need are then identical to those in the proof of Theorem 5.6. Indeed, for the wave estimates for α, we
argue exactly as in §8 and §8.2, except that we now have initial data on H0 instead of ˜Σ1; observe that
the data on H0 exactly have to be controlled in the norm (A.2). The bounds for the other geometric
quantities can be derived in the same manner as in §9.
To obtain a local solution, we first solve for the maximal globally hyperbolic future development of
the data (Σ, h, k) using [CBG69]. By standard domain of dependence argument (for instance in wave
coordinates), locally the solution is smoothly extendible up to an incoming null hypersurface H emanating
from ∂B1(0) in the initial data. The result of [Ren90] can then be used to solve the characteristic initial
value problem with data on H and H0, constructing a local solution to the mixed spacelike-characteristic
initial value problem.
□
Remark A.3. In (A.2), if we additionally require ν = 3 + δ
2, then a very similar proof shows that the
smallness of this norm propagates; see Remarks 8.14 and 9.3 for more details. This will in particular
allow us to show using the fundamental theorem of calculus and Cauchy–Schwarz that limr→∞(r5α)(u, r)
exists and that Bondi–Sachs peeling holds (see (1.8)).
Observe that there are a couple of key differences between the case of Cauchy data in Theorem 1.5 and
the case of the spacelike-characteristic initial value problem in Corollary A.2. First, in Theorem 1.5, if
one imposes a smallness condition for ν > 3, then this forces limr→∞(r5α)(1 −r, r) = 0 (see the norms
on the right-hand side of Corollary 8.7). On the other hand, in the setting of Corollary A.2, imposing
a smallness condition for ν > 3 is consistent with limr→∞(r5α)(u, r) being well-defined but non-trivial
(see the norms in (A.2)). Second, in Theorem 1.5, the decay rates of various initial data quantities are
constrained by the slowly decaying terms in Definition 1.3 (see also Remark 5.3), which restrict the decay
rates that can be propagated via the e4-transport equations. In contrast, in the setting of Corollary A.2,
the initialization of these transport equations occurs entirely on the compact region Σ = B1(0), and thus
such constraints do not exist (see also Remark 9.3).
Appendix B. Proof of theorem 5.2
In this section we show that bounds on the Cauchy data for the Einstein equations in suitable
(weighted) Sobolev spaces imply our initial data assumptions at the level of geometric quantities tied to
the Newman–Unti coordinates. We first need to establish some comparison results for these quantities
for solutions with initial data which are close.
